class="text-h3-headline ui-text-primary article-hero-alt-title" data-component="Title">FADE DEAD | Adventures in Reversing Malicious Run-Only AppleScripts</h1><p class="text-b1-body-400 ui-text-secondary article-hero-alt-description" data-component="Text">We show how to statically reverse run-only AppleScripts for the first time, and in the process reveal new IoCs of a long-running macOS Cryptominer campaign.</p><div class="article-hero-alt-footer"><div class="article-hero-alt-authors"><div class="text-b4-body-400 ui-text-secondary article-hero-alt-author" class="webui-toc-item"><a href="#resources" class="webui-toc-link" data-toc-id="resources">Resources</a></li></ul></div></nav></aside><div class="article-entry-main"><div class="text-rich-longform text-b3-body-400 ui-text-secondary"><h2 id="executive-summary">Executive Summary</h2><ul><li>macOS.OSAMiner is a cryptominer campaign that has resisted full researcher analysis for at least five years due to its use of multiple run-only AppleScripts.</li><li>macOS.OSAMiner has evolved to use a complex architecture, embedding one run-only AppleScript within another and retrieving further stages embedded in the source code of public-facing web pages.</li><li>Combining a public AppleScript disassembler repo with our own AEVT decompiler tool allowed us to statically reverse run-only AppleScripts for the first time and reveal previously unknown details about the campaign and the malware’s architecture.</li><li>We have released our <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://github.com/SentineLabs/aevt_decompile">AEVT decompiler</a> tool as open source to aid other researchers in the analysis of malicious run-only AppleScripts.</li></ul><h2 id="background">Background</h2><p>Back in 2018, reports surfaced on Chinese security sites[<a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.anquanke.com/post/id/160496">1</a>, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://translate.google.com/translate?hl=en&amp;sl=zh-CN&amp;u=https://www.codetd.com/article/2819752">2</a>] about a Monero mining trojan infecting macOS users. Symptoms included higher than usual CPU, system freeze and problems trying to open the system Activity Monitor.app. Investigations at the time concluded that macOS.OSAMiner, as we have dubbed it, had likely been circulating since 2015, distributed in popular cracked games and software such as League of Legends and MS Office.</p><p>Although some IoCs were retrieved from the wild and from dynamic execution by researchers, the fact that the malware authors used run-only AppleScripts prevented much further analysis. Indeed, <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.anquanke.com/member/121034">360 MeshFire Team</a> reported that the malicious applications:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt03ba5b0f09999caf/6aa3179ffe64ed9956ffd93e/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-21.35.33.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt03ba5b0f09999caf/6aa3179ffe64ed9956ffd93e/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-21.35.33.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt03ba5b0f09999caf/6aa3179ffe64ed9956ffd93e/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-21.35.33.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt03ba5b0f09999caf/6aa3179ffe64ed9956ffd93e/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-21.35.33.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt03ba5b0f09999caf/6aa3179ffe64ed9956ffd93e/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-21.35.33.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt03ba5b0f09999caf/6aa3179ffe64ed9956ffd93e/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-21.35.33.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>A similar conclusion was reached by another Chinese security researcher trying to dynamically analyse a different sample of macOS.OSAMiner in 2020 [<a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://translate.google.com/translate?hl=&amp;sl=auto&amp;tl=en&amp;u=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.tr0y.wang%2F2020%2F03%2F05%2FMacOS%E7%9A%84ssl4.plist%E6%8C%96%E7%9F%BF%E7%97%85%E6%AF%92%E6%8E%92%E6%9F%A5%E8%AE%B0%E5%BD%95%2F">3</a>], noting that “No reverse method has been found…so the investigation ends here”</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt7d6125c153afeaeb/6aa317a1ced7edbf37e88715/no-reverse-method.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt7d6125c153afeaeb/6aa317a1ced7edbf37e88715/no-reverse-method.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt7d6125c153afeaeb/6aa317a1ced7edbf37e88715/no-reverse-method.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt7d6125c153afeaeb/6aa317a1ced7edbf37e88715/no-reverse-method.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt7d6125c153afeaeb/6aa317a1ced7edbf37e88715/no-reverse-method.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt7d6125c153afeaeb/6aa317a1ced7edbf37e88715/no-reverse-method.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>In late 2020, we discovered that the malware authors, presumably building on their earlier success in evading full analysis, had continued to develop and evolve their techniques. Recent versions of macOS.OSAMiner add greater complexity by embedding one run-only AppleScript inside another, further complicating the already difficult process of analysis.</p><p>However, with the help of a little-known applescript-disassembler project and a decompiler tool we developed here at SentinelLabs, we have been able to reverse these samples and can now reveal for the first time their internal logic along with further IoCs used in the campaign.</p><p>We believe that the method we used here is generalizable to other run-only AppleScripts and we hope this research will be helpful to others in the security community when dealing with malware using the run-only AppleScript format.</p><h2 id="a-malicious-run-only-applescript-or-two">A Malicious Run-Only AppleScript (or Two)</h2><p>While malware hunting on VirusTotal, we came across the following property list:</p><p>com.apple.FY9.plist<br/><br/>9ad23b781a22085588dd32f5c0a1d7c5d2f6585b14f1369fd1ab056cb97b0702</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt30d79b819bdd8722/6aa317a20dada1942efcb2e5/com.apple_.4V.plist_.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt30d79b819bdd8722/6aa317a20dada1942efcb2e5/com.apple_.4V.plist_.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt30d79b819bdd8722/6aa317a20dada1942efcb2e5/com.apple_.4V.plist_.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt30d79b819bdd8722/6aa317a20dada1942efcb2e5/com.apple_.4V.plist_.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt30d79b819bdd8722/6aa317a20dada1942efcb2e5/com.apple_.4V.plist_.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt30d79b819bdd8722/6aa317a20dada1942efcb2e5/com.apple_.4V.plist_.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>As noted above, we have seen this before in 2018 and earlier in 2020. The older persistence agents are almost identical save for the labels and names of the targeted executable. In the 2018 version, the malware tries to disguise itself as belonging to both “apple.Google” and “apple.Yahoo”:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt55a78679d7484e8e/6aa317a465e4e9146cfc4862/Screenshot-2020-12-10-at-15.56.41.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt55a78679d7484e8e/6aa317a465e4e9146cfc4862/Screenshot-2020-12-10-at-15.56.41.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt55a78679d7484e8e/6aa317a465e4e9146cfc4862/Screenshot-2020-12-10-at-15.56.41.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt55a78679d7484e8e/6aa317a465e4e9146cfc4862/Screenshot-2020-12-10-at-15.56.41.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt55a78679d7484e8e/6aa317a465e4e9146cfc4862/Screenshot-2020-12-10-at-15.56.41.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt55a78679d7484e8e/6aa317a465e4e9146cfc4862/Screenshot-2020-12-10-at-15.56.41.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>The tell-tale LaunchAgent program argument is odd for its redundant use of <code>osascript</code> to call itself via a <code>do shell script</code> command (Lines 11-13). However, pivoting on the program argument, <code>com.apple.4V.plist</code>, led us to this newer sample for the executable:</p><p>df550039acad9e637c7c3ec2a629abf8b3f35faca18e58d447f490cf23f114e8</p><p>As with earlier versions of this malware, the executable also uses a <code>.plist</code> extension and runs from the user’s Library LaunchAgents folder and, again, <code>com.apple.4V.plist</code> is not a property list file but a run-only AppleScript:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blta6be23f59045e1d4/6aa317a593b5129347c49f7d/AppleScript-Compiled-1.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blta6be23f59045e1d4/6aa317a593b5129347c49f7d/AppleScript-Compiled-1.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blta6be23f59045e1d4/6aa317a593b5129347c49f7d/AppleScript-Compiled-1.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blta6be23f59045e1d4/6aa317a593b5129347c49f7d/AppleScript-Compiled-1.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blta6be23f59045e1d4/6aa317a593b5129347c49f7d/AppleScript-Compiled-1.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blta6be23f59045e1d4/6aa317a593b5129347c49f7d/AppleScript-Compiled-1.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>We can quickly confirm that this is a run-only AppleScript by attempting to decompile with <code>osadecompile</code>, which returns the error: <code>errOSASourceNotAvailable</code> (<a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.osstatus.com/search/results?platform=all&amp;framework=all&amp;search=-1756">-1756</a>)</p><h2 id="strings-may-tell-you-something-but-not-much">Strings May Tell You Something, But Not Much</h2><p>The best starting point with run-only scripts is to dump the strings and the hex. For strings, we generally find the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://github.com/fireeye/flare-floss">floss</a> tool to be superior to the macOS version of the <code>strings</code> command line tool. This sample proves to be a case in point, because what <code>strings</code> won’t show you but <code>floss</code> will is all the UTF-16 encoded hex that are buried in this file:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltcb099baef5b1e53a/6aa317a786429503b967831f/hexdump.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltcb099baef5b1e53a/6aa317a786429503b967831f/hexdump.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltcb099baef5b1e53a/6aa317a786429503b967831f/hexdump.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltcb099baef5b1e53a/6aa317a786429503b967831f/hexdump.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltcb099baef5b1e53a/6aa317a786429503b967831f/hexdump.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltcb099baef5b1e53a/6aa317a786429503b967831f/hexdump.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>At this point we should look at the hexdump.</p><p><code>% hexdump -C df550039acad9e637c7c3ec2a629abf8b3f35faca18e58d447f490cf23f114e8</code></p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blta6ae61f494452321/6aa317a84c39789a7d9f6e3f/hexdump-out.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blta6ae61f494452321/6aa317a84c39789a7d9f6e3f/hexdump-out.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blta6ae61f494452321/6aa317a84c39789a7d9f6e3f/hexdump-out.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blta6ae61f494452321/6aa317a84c39789a7d9f6e3f/hexdump-out.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Notice, in particular the magic header: <code>FasdUAS</code>, which is <code>46 61 73 64 55 41 53 20</code> in hex. Compare that to the embedded hex in the previous screenshot, or further down in our hexdump:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf578fff859646852/6aa317aaeb4b53202b88cb65/embedded-script.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf578fff859646852/6aa317aaeb4b53202b88cb65/embedded-script.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf578fff859646852/6aa317aaeb4b53202b88cb65/embedded-script.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>This shows that our run-only script has another run-only script embedded within it, encoded in hexadecimal, a trick that was not seen in the earlier variants of this malware.</p><p>One of the nice things about AppleScript is not only does it have a magic at the beginning of an AppleScript file it also has one to mark the end of the script:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt2fcc917663623bdf/6aa317ab638a887ee1e21c71/fade-dead.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt2fcc917663623bdf/6aa317ab638a887ee1e21c71/fade-dead.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt2fcc917663623bdf/6aa317ab638a887ee1e21c71/fade-dead.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" the end of the embedded script within the parent script by looking for the hex <code>fa de de ad</code> or <code>FADE DEAD</code>.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt030d0b2fc8ec0e72/6aa317ad0321bf69a5ac93af/embedded-fade-dead.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt030d0b2fc8ec0e72/6aa317ad0321bf69a5ac93af/embedded-fade-dead.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt030d0b2fc8ec0e72/6aa317ad0321bf69a5ac93af/embedded-fade-dead.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>We can now pull out all the code of the embedded script and dump that into a separate file.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6740b6565ac5a39b/6aa317aeac7d8f3256ee8849/xxd.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6740b6565ac5a39b/6aa317aeac7d8f3256ee8849/xxd.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6740b6565ac5a39b/6aa317aeac7d8f3256ee8849/xxd.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6740b6565ac5a39b/6aa317aeac7d8f3256ee8849/xxd.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6740b6565ac5a39b/6aa317aeac7d8f3256ee8849/xxd.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6740b6565ac5a39b/6aa317aeac7d8f3256ee8849/xxd.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>We can use <code>file</code> and <code>osadecompile</code> to confirm that we do indeed now have a second valid, run-only AppleScript:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt2d0324b41aee1c46/6aa317b08b27965db0161ceb/osadecompile.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt2d0324b41aee1c46/6aa317b08b27965db0161ceb/osadecompile.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt2d0324b41aee1c46/6aa317b08b27965db0161ceb/osadecompile.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt2d0324b41aee1c46/6aa317b08b27965db0161ceb/osadecompile.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt2d0324b41aee1c46/6aa317b08b27965db0161ceb/osadecompile.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Let’s now call <code>floss</code> on the extracted script and see what we have. You will see the output contains a lot of Apple Event (AEVT) codes and, at the end, a few UTF-16 encoded strings that were not revealed when we dumped the strings from the parent script:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltbff0dd35cf8c8aca/6aa317b3737c43fda97bf143/floss.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltbff0dd35cf8c8aca/6aa317b3737c43fda97bf143/floss.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltbff0dd35cf8c8aca/6aa317b3737c43fda97bf143/floss.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltbff0dd35cf8c8aca/6aa317b3737c43fda97bf143/floss.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltbff0dd35cf8c8aca/6aa317b3737c43fda97bf143/floss.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltbff0dd35cf8c8aca/6aa317b3737c43fda97bf143/floss.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Although the first image above does not quite show all the AEVT codes in the output, it’s easy to be distracted by the UTF-16 strings at the end, which immediately suggest something interesting: it looks like this script uses a <code>grep</code> search to find a particular process and kill it. It’s also clear the script is targeting both System Events.app and Activity Monitor. And there’s a tantalizing “Installe” string there, too!</p><p>The really interesting content of the script lies in the disassembly and the AEVT codes, but it’s difficult to see that from extracting the strings and a hexdump for two reasons:</p><ul><li>We don’t have any understanding of the structure or logic of the script</li><li>We don’t have human-readable translations of the AEVT codes.</li></ul><p>We will solve the first problem by using <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://github.com/Jinmo/applescript-disassembler">Jinmo’s applescript-disassembler</a> and the second problem by using our own <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://github.com/SentineLabs/aevt_decompile">aevt_decompile</a> tool.</p><h2 id="disassembling-run-only-applescripts">Disassembling Run-only AppleScripts</h2><p>We have two targets for disassembly, the parent script and the embedded script. Let’s start with the parent.</p><p>Once you’ve installed and built the applescript-disassembler project, simply call the target script against the <code>disassembly.py</code> script and output to a text file for analysis:</p><p><code>% ./disassembly.py df550039acad9e637c7c3ec2a629abf8b3f35faca18e58d447f490cf23f114e8 &gt; parent.txt</code></p><p>The beginning of the <code>parent.txt</code> file should look something like this:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt3599b64ab7ae0470/6aa317b40dada1dacffcb2ed/data-offset-2.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt3599b64ab7ae0470/6aa317b40dada1dacffcb2ed/data-offset-2.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt3599b64ab7ae0470/6aa317b40dada1dacffcb2ed/data-offset-2.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt3599b64ab7ae0470/6aa317b40dada1dacffcb2ed/data-offset-2.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt3599b64ab7ae0470/6aa317b40dada1dacffcb2ed/data-offset-2.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt3599b64ab7ae0470/6aa317b40dada1dacffcb2ed/data-offset-2.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>The first thing to note is that the content is divided into functions, separated by the lines</p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>=== data offset  ===
Function name :
Function arguments:
</code></pre><p>These correspond to AppleScript <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://developer.apple.com/library/archive/documentation/AppleScript/Conceptual/AppleScriptLangGuide/conceptual/ASLR_about_handlers.html">handlers</a>. In this compiled script, there are three named handlers and one unnamed handler, which corresponds to the script’s “main” handler (i.e., the main function called on execution).</p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>=== data offset 2 ===
Function name : e
Function arguments:  [&#x27;_s&#x27;]
</code></pre><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>=== data offset 3 ===
Function name : d
Function arguments:  [&#x27;_s&#x27;]
</code></pre><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>=== data offset 4 ===
Function name : r_t
Function arguments:  [&#x27;t_t&#x27;, &#x27;s_s&#x27;, &#x27;r_s&#x27;]
</code></pre><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>=== data offset 5 ===
Function name : &lt;Value type=object value=&lt;Value type=event_identifier value=&#x27;AEVT&#x27;-&#x27;oapp&#x27;-&#x27;null&#x27;-&#x27;x00x00x80x00&#x27;-&#x27;****&#x27;-&#x27;x00x00x90x00&#x27;&gt;&gt;
Function arguments:  &lt;empty or unknown&gt;
</code></pre><p>The most interesting function for us at the moment is the second function, ‘d’, which we will rename as the ‘decode’ function. This function is called multiple times later in the code and passed an obfuscated string of hex characters. Reversing this function will allow us to see the obfuscated strings in plain text. Even better, since the same function is used in all samples we’ve come across since 2018, it’ll also allow us to decode the strings right across the campaign and observe how it has changed.</p><p>The disassembler conveniently comments where this function is called. To find the first call, search for a PositionalMessageSend (i.e., handler call) with the name  ‘d’.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf83b8d6d267463af/6aa317b565e4e9dedbfc486a/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.05.03.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf83b8d6d267463af/6aa317b565e4e9dedbfc486a/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.05.03.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf83b8d6d267463af/6aa317b565e4e9dedbfc486a/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.05.03.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf83b8d6d267463af/6aa317b565e4e9dedbfc486a/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.05.03.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf83b8d6d267463af/6aa317b565e4e9dedbfc486a/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.05.03.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf83b8d6d267463af/6aa317b565e4e9dedbfc486a/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.05.03.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>For example, the following hex string at offset <strong>000d4</strong> is passed to the decode function at <strong>000d8</strong>:</p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>&#x27;x00xd4x00xd6x00xcdx00xd2x00xd8x00xcax00x84x00x8bx00x89x00xc6x00x8bx00x84x00x8b&#x27;</code></pre><p>Note that in the decode handler, there is a loop which iterates over each hexadecimal byte code and then subtracts <code>x64</code> from it.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0c1f56a6533c3234/6aa317b71974280889cf5589/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.06.28.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0c1f56a6533c3234/6aa317b71974280889cf5589/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.06.28.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0c1f56a6533c3234/6aa317b71974280889cf5589/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.06.28.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0c1f56a6533c3234/6aa317b71974280889cf5589/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.06.28.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0c1f56a6533c3234/6aa317b71974280889cf5589/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.06.28.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0c1f56a6533c3234/6aa317b71974280889cf5589/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.06.28.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>It then returns that number as an ASCII code, concatenating each result to produce a UTF-8 string (note the input is padded with <code>x00</code>, indicating a UTF-16 string, but the function ignores any values that are not greater than zero). The first line of input hex is returned from the decode handler as the following UTF-8 string:</p><p><code>printf &#x27;%b&#x27; &#x27;</code></p><p>Based on this, it’s easy enough to implement our own decode function to deobfuscate all the obfuscated strings in the run-only scripts. We add this logic to our <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://github.com/SentineLabs/aevt_decompile">aevt_decompile</a> tool as discussed further below.</p><p>The handler ‘e’ is never called in the malware code, but inspection reveals it to be the reverse of the ‘d’ function. In other words, the function is used to encode plain UTF-8 strings to produce the obfuscated hex and is presumably used by the authors when building their malware.</p><p>The function ‘r_t’, which takes three parameters, is only called once. This function takes a target, a source and a ‘delimiter’.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt644c84ee815d68dd/6aa317b80321bf812bac93b3/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.07.37.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt644c84ee815d68dd/6aa317b80321bf812bac93b3/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.07.37.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt644c84ee815d68dd/6aa317b80321bf812bac93b3/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.07.37.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt644c84ee815d68dd/6aa317b80321bf812bac93b3/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.07.37.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt644c84ee815d68dd/6aa317b80321bf812bac93b3/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.07.37.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt644c84ee815d68dd/6aa317b80321bf812bac93b3/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.07.37.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Once we substitute the constant hex values shown in the disassembler for the Apple Event codes (discussed below), we will see that its purpose is to find a target substring by separating the source string into components divided by the delimiter. From our analysis below, it appears that this handler is used to format the embedded AppleScript before writing it out to file.</p><p>The fourth, nameless, function is in fact where all the executable code is called from in an AppleScript (think of it like a ‘main’ function in other languages). Again, we’ll discuss this further below when we move on to decompiling the Apple Event codes and annotating the output of the disassembler.</p><h2 id="disassembling-the-embedded-applescript">Disassembling the Embedded AppleScript</h2><p><code>% ./disassembly.py f145fce4089360f1bc9f9fb7f95a8f202d5b840eac9baab9e72d8f4596772de9 &gt;  em.txt</code></p><p>The embedded run-only AppleScript also contains four functions, ‘e’, ‘d’, ‘kPro’ and the nameless ‘main’ function where the script’s executable code is called. The first two are duplicates of the encode and decode functions in the parent script.</p><p>The ‘kPro’ is obviously a ‘killProcess’ function. We can determine this directly from the disassembler as much of the functionality is revealed as hardcoded strings:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt13bea9462afb4a1f/6aa317bad2fcfb58c7474bc5/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.09.20.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt13bea9462afb4a1f/6aa317bad2fcfb58c7474bc5/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.09.20.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt13bea9462afb4a1f/6aa317bad2fcfb58c7474bc5/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.09.20.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt13bea9462afb4a1f/6aa317bad2fcfb58c7474bc5/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.09.20.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt13bea9462afb4a1f/6aa317bad2fcfb58c7474bc5/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.09.20.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt13bea9462afb4a1f/6aa317bad2fcfb58c7474bc5/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.09.20.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>We will automate extraction of these strings in our decompiler below, but for now note that the code above contains the following embedded strings:</p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>ps ax | grep
grep -v grep | awk ‘{print $1 }’
kill -9
</code></pre><p>The function is passed the name of a process as a string, which is concatenated to produce the shell command:</p><p><code>ps ax | grep &lt;name&gt; | grep -v grep | awk ‘{print $1}’</code></p><p>This command is then executed via the AppleScript <code>do shell script</code> command. If the command returns a PID for the process name, a further <code>do shell script</code> command is executed to kill the PID.</p><p>We can see that the ‘killProcess’ function is called twice in the code. On the first call, it is passed a string concatenated from “Activity Monitor” and “.app”, both of which are hardcoded in the source:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf68255881ac4a94a/6aa317bbfe64ed8edaffd948/killProcess-handler.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf68255881ac4a94a/6aa317bbfe64ed8edaffd948/killProcess-handler.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf68255881ac4a94a/6aa317bbfe64ed8edaffd948/killProcess-handler.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf68255881ac4a94a/6aa317bbfe64ed8edaffd948/killProcess-handler.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf68255881ac4a94a/6aa317bbfe64ed8edaffd948/killProcess-handler.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf68255881ac4a94a/6aa317bbfe64ed8edaffd948/killProcess-handler.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>This call occurs only if “Activity Monitor” is returned in the list of System Events’ currently running processes.</p><p>The second call to ‘killProcess’ requires decoding a number of the script’s obfuscated hexadecimal strings by passing those through the ‘d’ or decode function as we did before.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt89d5d6084b52a36f/6aa317bdedddfb5abacbe72f/killPro.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt89d5d6084b52a36f/6aa317bdedddfb5abacbe72f/killPro.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt89d5d6084b52a36f/6aa317bdedddfb5abacbe72f/killPro.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt89d5d6084b52a36f/6aa317bdedddfb5abacbe72f/killPro.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt89d5d6084b52a36f/6aa317bdedddfb5abacbe72f/killPro.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt89d5d6084b52a36f/6aa317bdedddfb5abacbe72f/killPro.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Here we show some of the output of the <code>disassembler.py</code> script after running it through our decompiler tool, discussed in the next section:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte036441404f890af/6aa317be737c43c9f87bf147/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.15.27.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte036441404f890af/6aa317be737c43c9f87bf147/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.15.27.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte036441404f890af/6aa317be737c43c9f87bf147/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.15.27.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte036441404f890af/6aa317be737c43c9f87bf147/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.15.27.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte036441404f890af/6aa317be737c43c9f87bf147/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.15.27.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p><code>ps ax | grep -E &#x27;360|Keeper|MacMgr|Lemon|Malware|Avast|Avira|CleanMyMac&#x27; | grep -v grep | awk &#x27;{print $1}&#x27;</code></p><h2 id="building-a-decompiler-on-top-of-the-disassembler">Building a Decompiler on Top of the Disassembler</h2><p>Without the AEVT codes and other decompiling, the output of the disassembler is obscure at best.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9fe28d071de882f6/6aa317c0737c430cfa7bf14f/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.03.09-scaled.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9fe28d071de882f6/6aa317c0737c430cfa7bf14f/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.03.09-scaled.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9fe28d071de882f6/6aa317c0737c430cfa7bf14f/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.03.09-scaled.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9fe28d071de882f6/6aa317c0737c430cfa7bf14f/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.03.09-scaled.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9fe28d071de882f6/6aa317c0737c430cfa7bf14f/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.03.09-scaled.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9fe28d071de882f6/6aa317c0737c430cfa7bf14f/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.03.09-scaled.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Running our decompile tool on the output from the disassembler, however, makes things much clearer. Not only do we get each AEVT code’s command name and description in human readable form, our tool also automatically extracts and decodes the malware’s obfuscated hex strings.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdb153668a2e00efd/6aa317c14c39787c409f6e43/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.18.56.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, strings, as well as hardcoded strings and number formats are also translated.</p><p>From the disassembler output we get:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" running it through the decompiler, we get a much more informative output for these lines:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte820dcd376962185/6aa317c4ced7ed5740e88719/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.21.39.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte820dcd376962185/6aa317c4ced7ed5740e88719/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.21.39.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte820dcd376962185/6aa317c4ced7ed5740e88719/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.21.39.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte820dcd376962185/6aa317c4ced7ed5740e88719/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.21.39.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Similarly, “Tell” blocks in AppleScript are much easier to understand after running the decompiler. From the disassembly alone, it’s difficult to interpret the purpose of the following code between offset <strong>00007</strong> and <strong>00018</strong>, for example:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9dd6627d7dca61f7/6aa317c68b2796dcba161cef/tell-block.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9dd6627d7dca61f7/6aa317c68b2796dcba161cef/tell-block.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9dd6627d7dca61f7/6aa317c68b2796dcba161cef/tell-block.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9dd6627d7dca61f7/6aa317c68b2796dcba161cef/tell-block.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9dd6627d7dca61f7/6aa317c68b2796dcba161cef/tell-block.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt9dd6627d7dca61f7/6aa317c68b2796dcba161cef/tell-block.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>The decompiler makes it clear that the block targets System Events and returns the disk capacity of “/”, the startup drive.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdbbdf5ae29d3dd43/6aa317c78b27966d58161cf3/tell-block-interpreted.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdbbdf5ae29d3dd43/6aa317c78b27966d58161cf3/tell-block-interpreted.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdbbdf5ae29d3dd43/6aa317c78b27966d58161cf3/tell-block-interpreted.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdbbdf5ae29d3dd43/6aa317c78b27966d58161cf3/tell-block-interpreted.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdbbdf5ae29d3dd43/6aa317c78b27966d58161cf3/tell-block-interpreted.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdbbdf5ae29d3dd43/6aa317c78b27966d58161cf3/tell-block-interpreted.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Our tool attempts to return the human-readable code for an AEVT from all available sources. That can mean multiple interpretations for a single line.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt217ad564683a7866/6aa317c9e0b3c6daff1dcae2/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.41.30-scaled.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt217ad564683a7866/6aa317c9e0b3c6daff1dcae2/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.41.30-scaled.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt217ad564683a7866/6aa317c9e0b3c6daff1dcae2/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.41.30-scaled.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt217ad564683a7866/6aa317c9e0b3c6daff1dcae2/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.41.30-scaled.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt217ad564683a7866/6aa317c9e0b3c6daff1dcae2/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.41.30-scaled.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt217ad564683a7866/6aa317c9e0b3c6daff1dcae2/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-03.41.30-scaled.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Years of experience with AppleScript has taught us that this kind of verbosity is vital to make sense of complex scripts, where the meaning of AEVT codes can change depending on the target of the block they appear in. For those less familiar with the vagaries of AppleScript, a little explanation here may be in order.</p><h2 id="interlude-a-quick-guide-to-aevt-codes">Interlude: A Quick Guide to AEVT Codes</h2><p>According to Apple’s legacy documentation (I maintain a PDF repository <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://applescriptlibrary.wordpress.com/">here</a>), Apple Event codes <em>“are defined primarily in the header files AppleEvents.h and AERegistry.h in the AE framework”</em>. However, the word “primarily” is an important, and arguably misleading, qualifier as there are many other places where the codes can be defined, depending on exactly what the script targets.</p><p>Most AppleScripts will likely make use of the <code>StandardAdditions.osax</code>, which defines a whole range of codes that add essential functionality to the base AppleScript language. In addition, malware scripts are likely to also target either or both of System Events and the Terminal, both of which have their own definitions for Apple Event codes. Indeed, any application that is “scriptable” can define its own Apple Event codes. These definitions are nowadays located in an XML file with the extension <code>.sdef</code> inside each application’s own bundle Resources folder.</p><p>Because of this architecture, you can only retrieve the codes for an AppleScript if you have the targeted applications on your system. Fortunately, in the case of malware, it is highly likely that the malware will only target system applications that can be found on every Mac, such as System Events and the Terminal, both because of their power to manipulate the system and because of their universality – an AppleScript that targets an application that is not on the victim’s system will fail to execute fully or at all, and will thus have limited utility, at least for commodity malware.</p><p>The paths we need for most Apple Event codes then can be found in the following locations:</p><p><strong>AEFramework:</strong><br/><br/>We need the source for the AEFramework headers, and that requires installation of the Xcode Command Line tools. These should be found within the <code>/Library/Developer/CommandLineTools/SDK</code>s folder. For example on Catalina:</p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>/Library/Developer/CommandLineTools/SDKs/MacOSX10.15.sdk/System/Library/Frameworks/CoreServices.framework/Versions/A/Frameworks/AE.framework/Versions/A/Headers</code></pre><p>For Big Sur,</p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>/Library/Developer/CommandLineTools/SDKs/MacOSX11.0.sdk/System/Library/Frameworks/CoreServices.framework/Versions/A/Frameworks/AE.framework/Versions/A/Headers/AERegistry.h.</code></pre><p>Alternatively, you may find the path to these from the Terminal, via</p><p><code>% xcode-select -p</code></p><p>The output of that command can then be extended with the following path that should take you to the Headers directory:</p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>/Platforms/MacOSX.platform/Developer/SDKs/MacOSX.sdk/System/Library/Frameworks/CoreServices.framework/Versions/A/Frameworks/AE.framework/Versions/A/Headers</code></pre><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltb25914ef18dd2781/6aa317cb79617b2a66867fed/xcode-select.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltb25914ef18dd2781/6aa317cb79617b2a66867fed/xcode-select.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltb25914ef18dd2781/6aa317cb79617b2a66867fed/xcode-select.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltb25914ef18dd2781/6aa317cb79617b2a66867fed/xcode-select.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltb25914ef18dd2781/6aa317cb79617b2a66867fed/xcode-select.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltb25914ef18dd2781/6aa317cb79617b2a66867fed/xcode-select.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p><strong>AppleScriptKit:</strong></p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>/System/Library/Frameworks/AppleScriptKit.framework/Versions/A/Resources/AppleScriptKit.sdef</code></pre><p><strong>Standard Additions OSAX:</strong></p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>/System/Library/ScriptingAdditions/StandardAdditions.osax/Contents/Resources/StandardAdditions.sdef</code></pre><p><strong>System Events.app:</strong></p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>/System/Library/CoreServices/System Events.app/Contents/Resources/SystemEvents.sdef</code></pre><p><strong>Terminal.app:</strong></p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>/System/Applications/Utilities/Terminal.app/Contents/Resources/Terminal.sdef</code></pre><p>There are, of course, many more <code>.sdef</code> files on any given system – as many as there are scriptable applications on the current OS installation. The Script Editor’s Dictionary viewer lists all scriptable applications on a system:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltd889c681a58e7cd2/6aa317ccac7d8f51c2ee884f/open-dictionary.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltd889c681a58e7cd2/6aa317ccac7d8f51c2ee884f/open-dictionary.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltd889c681a58e7cd2/6aa317ccac7d8f51c2ee884f/open-dictionary.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltd889c681a58e7cd2/6aa317ccac7d8f51c2ee884f/open-dictionary.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltd889c681a58e7cd2/6aa317ccac7d8f51c2ee884f/open-dictionary.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltd889c681a58e7cd2/6aa317ccac7d8f51c2ee884f/open-dictionary.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>However, few of those will be targeted by malware. Even so, since the scripting definition file appears in a predictable location within each application bundle, our decompiler attempts to suggest further SDEFs for other applications targeted in the script if they exist on the analysis machine. Which code is the correct one given the context of the rest of the script is up to the analyst to interpret. The aim of our decompiler is to make this fairly easy to discern.</p><h2 id="understanding-the-macos-osaminer-campaign">Understanding the macOS.OSAMiner Campaign</h2><p>With these tools to hand, our workflow will be as follows:</p><pre class="hjs-code-block" data-component="CodeBlock"><code>% disassembler.py target.scpt &gt; target.txt
% aevt_decompile target.txt
 -&gt; ~/Desktop/target.out
</code></pre><p>The aevt_decompile program will by default output to <code>~/Desktop/&lt;filename&gt;.out</code> (e.g.,  target.out), though this can be changed in the code. The <code>.out</code> file can be opened or read in Vi, BBEdit or whatever happens to be your preferred text editor.</p><p>Running our tools on a number of samples from 2018 to 2020 now reveals more clearly how the macos.OSAMiner campaign works. The parent script first checks the disk capacity of the victim’s machine via System Events and exits if there is not enough free space.</p><p>Next, it writes out the embedded AppleScript to <code>~/Library/k.plist</code> via a <code>do shell script</code> command, and then executes the embedded script with <code>osascript</code>, again shelling out via <code>do shell script</code>. As we shall see, the primary function of this embedded script is to take on evasion and anti-analysis duties.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt24d1cd4e42d3a7c1/6aa317ce62b362857147146d/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.32.47.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt24d1cd4e42d3a7c1/6aa317ce62b362857147146d/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.32.47.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt24d1cd4e42d3a7c1/6aa317ce62b362857147146d/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.32.47.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt24d1cd4e42d3a7c1/6aa317ce62b362857147146d/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.32.47.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt24d1cd4e42d3a7c1/6aa317ce62b362857147146d/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.32.47.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt24d1cd4e42d3a7c1/6aa317ce62b362857147146d/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.32.47.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>After writing out the embedded script, the parent script continues to execute, setting up a persistence agent and downloading the first stage of the miner by retrieving a URL embedded in a public web page.</p><p>In our particular sample, the obfuscated, hardcoded URL is</p><p><code>hxxp://www[.]budaybu10000[.]com:8080</code></p><p>However, this URL currently does not resolve, which suggests either that the malware campaign for this particular URL has not been activated yet or for some reason has gone offline. Fortunately, we can use our disassembler and decompiler on other samples to find a still live URL and see what it serves. In this case, we can find the following URL</p><p><code>hxxps://www[.]emoneyspace[.]com/wodaywo</code></p><p>having the same function in an older sample (ab4596d3f8347d447051eb4e4075e04c37ce161514b4ce3fae91010aac7ae97f) and still live. The URL takes us to the following public web page:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt163688cf2761ddb9/6aa317cf344c200cd77fab6e/download-site.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt163688cf2761ddb9/6aa317cf344c200cd77fab6e/download-site.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt163688cf2761ddb9/6aa317cf344c200cd77fab6e/download-site.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt163688cf2761ddb9/6aa317cf344c200cd77fab6e/download-site.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt163688cf2761ddb9/6aa317cf344c200cd77fab6e/download-site.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt163688cf2761ddb9/6aa317cf344c200cd77fab6e/download-site.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>The source code of the webpage is parsed by the malware to retrieve an embedded URL surrounded by the text delimiters <code>-=-=-=</code>. Curiously, the code for extracting the URL is duplicated inline rather than being passed off to the ‘r_t’ function at data offset <strong>4</strong>.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0fa906f567561b83/6aa317d1e0b3c627cc1dcae6/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.37.37.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0fa906f567561b83/6aa317d1e0b3c627cc1dcae6/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.37.37.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0fa906f567561b83/6aa317d1e0b3c627cc1dcae6/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.37.37.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0fa906f567561b83/6aa317d1e0b3c627cc1dcae6/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.37.37.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0fa906f567561b83/6aa317d1e0b3c627cc1dcae6/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.37.37.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt0fa906f567561b83/6aa317d1e0b3c627cc1dcae6/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.37.37.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>The extracted URL is passed to the <code>curl</code> utility for downloading a remote file. Despite the <code>.png</code> extension, it is of course another run-only AppleScript, which is now written out to <code>~/Library/11.png</code> on the infected device and executed at offset <strong>00387</strong>.</p><p>The malware now has four components running (path names can vary across samples):</p><ul><li>a persistence agent for the parent script at <code>~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.apple.FY9.plist</code></li><li>the parent script executing from <code>~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.apple.4V.plist</code></li><li>the embedded evasion/anti-analysis AppleScript running from <code>~/Library/k.plist</code></li><li>the miner setup script running from <code>~/Library/11.png</code></li></ul><p>Before we turn to the latter two, note that the parent script has not finished its business yet. It continues to execute various tasks, including gathering the device serial number, restarting the <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://ss64.com/osx/launchctl.html">launchctl</a> job and killing the Terminal application. This last action is one of the few that are not executed through <code>do shell script</code> commands; instead, the script targets the Terminal directly through its own <code>do script</code> AppleScript command.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt889c5dcb9158d8f2/6aa317d2024c450cd7d213ad/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.39.36.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt889c5dcb9158d8f2/6aa317d2024c450cd7d213ad/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.39.36.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt889c5dcb9158d8f2/6aa317d2024c450cd7d213ad/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.39.36.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt889c5dcb9158d8f2/6aa317d2024c450cd7d213ad/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.39.36.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt889c5dcb9158d8f2/6aa317d2024c450cd7d213ad/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.39.36.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt889c5dcb9158d8f2/6aa317d2024c450cd7d213ad/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.39.36.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Meanwhile, the embedded AppleScript looks for the Activity Monitor process among System Events’ process list. If found, it passes the application’s name to its ‘kPro’ or ‘killProcess’ handler to prevent the user inspecting resource usage.</p><p>Even more interesting, the embedded script also functions to perform evasion tasks from certain consumer-level monitoring and clean up tools. It searches both for PIDs among running processes and it parses the operating system’s <code>install.log</code> for apps matching its hardcoded list, killing any that it finds along the way.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte036441404f890af/6aa317be737c43c9f87bf147/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.15.27.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte036441404f890af/6aa317be737c43c9f87bf147/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.15.27.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte036441404f890af/6aa317be737c43c9f87bf147/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.15.27.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte036441404f890af/6aa317be737c43c9f87bf147/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.15.27.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blte036441404f890af/6aa317be737c43c9f87bf147/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.15.27.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt02fc0c7844629d04/6aa317d4edddfb8169cbe733/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.41.57.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt02fc0c7844629d04/6aa317d4edddfb8169cbe733/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.41.57.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt02fc0c7844629d04/6aa317d4edddfb8169cbe733/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.41.57.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt02fc0c7844629d04/6aa317d4edddfb8169cbe733/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.41.57.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><h2 id="downloading-and-configuring-the-miner-component">Downloading and Configuring the Miner Component</h2><p>Finally, running our tools on the miner setup script reveals that it functions as a downloader and config for what appears to be an instance of the open-source  <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://github.com/fireice-uk/xmr-stak">XMR-STAK-RX – Free Monero RandomX Miner</a> software.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdd2f9ed8a3ca453e/6aa317d6197428db08cf558d/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-16.19.23.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdd2f9ed8a3ca453e/6aa317d6197428db08cf558d/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-16.19.23.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdd2f9ed8a3ca453e/6aa317d6197428db08cf558d/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-16.19.23.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdd2f9ed8a3ca453e/6aa317d6197428db08cf558d/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-16.19.23.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdd2f9ed8a3ca453e/6aa317d6197428db08cf558d/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-16.19.23.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltdd2f9ed8a3ca453e/6aa317d6197428db08cf558d/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-16.19.23.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>The setup script includes pool address, password and other configuration information but no wallet address.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt769ed485f5fffe9f/6aa317d7b02f7ddf260eca06/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.44.17.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt769ed485f5fffe9f/6aa317d7b02f7ddf260eca06/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.44.17.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt769ed485f5fffe9f/6aa317d7b02f7ddf260eca06/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.44.17.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt769ed485f5fffe9f/6aa317d7b02f7ddf260eca06/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.44.17.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6decc8b9e7addf7c/6aa317d93523640154f454b8/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.45.16.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6decc8b9e7addf7c/6aa317d93523640154f454b8/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.45.16.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6decc8b9e7addf7c/6aa317d93523640154f454b8/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.45.16.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6decc8b9e7addf7c/6aa317d93523640154f454b8/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.45.16.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt6decc8b9e7addf7c/6aa317d93523640154f454b8/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.45.16.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>This miner script also checks to ensure there is enough disk space, but this time using the Unix utility <code>df</code> rather than the System Events application.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt067d18d3d8a3a722/6aa317daeb9443339694954f/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.46.38.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt067d18d3d8a3a722/6aa317daeb9443339694954f/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.46.38.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt067d18d3d8a3a722/6aa317daeb9443339694954f/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.46.38.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt067d18d3d8a3a722/6aa317daeb9443339694954f/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.46.38.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt067d18d3d8a3a722/6aa317daeb9443339694954f/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.46.38.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt067d18d3d8a3a722/6aa317daeb9443339694954f/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.46.38.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>The miner setup script also uses the built-in <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://ss64.com/osx/caffeinate.html">caffeinate</a> tool to prevent the Mac sleeping and also does some evasion checks. It parses the output of the built-in <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://ss64.com/osx/system_profiler.html">system_profiler</a> tool to check whether the device has 4 cores, a rudimentary way of trying to ensure it is not running in a virtual machine environment.</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf2ea6f905d45e98c/6aa317dc197428f2f4cf5591/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.51.14.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf2ea6f905d45e98c/6aa317dc197428f2f4cf5591/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.51.14.jpg?width=768&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 768w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf2ea6f905d45e98c/6aa317dc197428f2f4cf5591/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.51.14.jpg?width=1280&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1280w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf2ea6f905d45e98c/6aa317dc197428f2f4cf5591/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.51.14.jpg?width=1920&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 1920w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf2ea6f905d45e98c/6aa317dc197428f2f4cf5591/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.51.14.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltf2ea6f905d45e98c/6aa317dc197428f2f4cf5591/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.51.14.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p>Next, a folder is created in <code>~/Library/Caches/</code> with the name “com.apple.” and two uppercase letters, which are hardcoded in the script. In this sample, those letters are ‘CM’, so the folder to be written is <code>~/Library/Caches/com.apple.CM/</code>.</p><p>Interestingly, we can see from reversing an <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/f89205a8091584e1215cf33854ad764939008004a688b7e530b085e3230effce/detection">older sample</a> with our tools that previously the malware wrote its components to the <code>~/Library/Safari/</code> folder, but as that is now prohibited by <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.sentinelone.com/blog/mojaves-security-hardening-user-protections-bypassed/">TCC restrictions</a> since Mojave 10.14, the malware authors have clearly had to adapt.</p><p>Various files are written to this folder:</p><ul><li>config.txt</li><li>cpu.txt</li><li>pools.txt</li><li>ssl.zip</li></ul><p>The last is a compressed folder which contains a file variously called ssl.plist, ssl3.plist, ssl4.plist and so on. In keeping with the malware’s tactic of using misleading file extensions, this is of course not a plist but in fact a <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.sentinelone.com/blog/how-to-reverse-macos-malware-part-two/">Mach-O</a> executable. The executable appears to be an instance of the XMR-STAK miner and is downloaded from a hardcoded and obfuscated URL:</p><p><img alt="" loading="lazy" width="800" height="450" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="rich-text-asset" style="color:transparent" sizes="(max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" srcSet="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/blt89ca7562ed914bea/6aa317dd65e4e9832cfc4872/Screenshot-2021-01-06-at-22.53.58.jpg?width=375&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 375w, https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltcbcfe405e339d7a8/6aa317e013fa1f2fe789ebee/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-16.38.13.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto 2560w" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/blt53c99b43892c2378/bltcbcfe405e339d7a8/6aa317e013fa1f2fe789ebee/Screenshot-2021-01-05-at-16.38.13.jpg?width=2560&amp;quality=90&amp;format=auto"/></p><p><code>97febb1aa15ad7b1c321f056f7164526eb698297e0fea0c23bd127498ba3e9bb</code>  <a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/97febb1aa15ad7b1c321f056f7164526eb698297e0fea0c23bd127498ba3e9bb/content/strings">ssl4.plist</a></p><h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2><p>Run-only AppleScripts are surprisingly rare in the macOS malware world, but both the longevity of and the lack of attention to the macOS.OSAMiner campaign, which has likely been running for at least 5 years, shows exactly how powerful run-only AppleScripts can be for evasion and anti-analysis. In this case, we have not seen the actor use any of the more powerful features of AppleScript that we’ve discussed elsewhere [<a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.sentinelone.com/blog/macos-red-team-calling-apple-apis-without-building-binaries/">4</a>,<a target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer" href="https://www.sentinelone.com/blog/how-offensive-actors-use-applescript-for-attacking-macos/">5</a>], but that is an attack vector that remains wide open and which many defensive tools are not equipped to handle. In the event that other threat actors begin picking up on the utility of leveraging run-only AppleScripts, we hope this research and the tools discussed above will prove to be of use to analysts.</p><h2 id="hashes-and-iocs">Hashes and IoCs</h2><p>SHA1: d760c99dec3efd98e3166881d327aa2f4a8735ef<br/><br/>SHA256:
